227. HENRY BUTCHER was again indicted for a like offence.
BENJAMIN WILLIS. I paid the prisoner, on the 21st of September, 6l. 19s. 5d.; he gave me a discharge for it - I always found him a very upright young man.
COURT to BENJAMIN NICHOLAS WILLIAMS. Q. Have you any thing more to add? A. No; but I have a paper here of the prisoner's hand-writing.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was not that after your brother had said he had better give the account? A. Yes.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271206-227]
228. JAMES GROVER was indicted, for that he, on the 19th of November, at St. Dunstan in the West, in and upon John Williams, feloniously, wilfully, and maliciously, did make an assault, and with a certain sharp instrument, feloniously, &c. did stab and cut the said John Williams, in and upon the left arm of the said John Williams, with intent, feloniously, &c. to kill and murder him, against the statute, &c.
SECOND COUNT, with intent to disable him, &c.
THIRD COUNT, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
JOHN WILLIAMS. I am a tailor, and live at No. 9, Mark's-place, Fetter-lane. On the 19th of November, about seven o'clock in the evening, I heard a disturbance of several persons, as I thought, running down the place, where I live; I opened the door to see who it might be, as there is no thoroughfare. I saw the prisoner at the bar, pursuing his wife; he at that time lived in the court, on the opposite side of the way. I did not at that time see that he had any thing in his hand - I only saw him and his wife; when I opened my door, he turned, came to me, and said "You bl-y rogue, I will murder you," and shoved a knife as I suppose it to be into my left arm.
Q. Was that the first word you heard uttered? A. Yes; I found I was stabbed; I put my hand to my arm, and the blood was gushing out; he attempted several times forward with the instrument which he had in his hand to stab me again. I stepped back into the house, and put my hand a little behind me, where I knew there was a yard measure, and took that in my own defence; he did not come in - he was at the door; I struck him with the yard, and drove him back into the court; he still continued thrusting at me, till I broke the yard measure into short pieces, in my hand; he then ran away into his own house. I heard him say he would stab his wife also - he would murder she also. I sent for an officer, who took him, and a surgeon, who dressed my wound.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You are comehere to prosecute this man for his life? A. I am come to prosecute him according to law, as he deserves.
Q. How long have you known his wife? A. Which way do you mean? I have known her as a woman, apparently for about a year and a half.
Q. Had not this man forbid you his house? A. I forgot to state that, upon the 6th of October, in a public-house, he threatened to stab me with a dagger; and on the Sunday night previous to this, I heard him threaten my life in his own house.
COURT. Q. What did he say? A. He said in the public-house, that he had bought a dagger on purpose to run through my heart.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Before the day he struck you with a knife, had he not forbid you his house? A. Yes; on the 8th of October - he struck me on the 19th of November; I had been to his house frequently in his absence, and in his presence, for my washing.
Q. Had you not frequently drank tea, and breakfasted with his wife in his absence? A. Yes; and dined there too; she dressed it for me and my family - I have no wife, I have some boys. I dined there in person - the dinner was sent to my house for them.
Q. Upon your solemn oath, did you ever introduce this man's wife as Mrs. Williams, to any one? A. I never did I never called her Mrs. Williams in any body's presence, I swear that. I know Kearsley and his wife; I was at the Red Lion public-house, Shoe-lane, in their company, in the Bartholomew fair week. I did not introduce the prisoner's wife to them as my wife, nor call her Mrs. Williams in their presence. I know Renshaw, she is a married woman; I never called the prisoner's wife Mrs. Williams in her company, nor introduce her as Mrs. Williams. I know a woman named Bosley; I never introduced the prisoner's wife to her as Mrs. Williams - they were all in company.
Q. Did you at any public-house, in the presence of each and all these people, call her Mrs. Williams; in the Bartholomew fair week? A. I never did.
Q. How late did you stay at the prisoner's house in his absence? A. Perhaps till ten or eleven o'clock at night, for little odd things I might want. I am not a follower of Mr. Carlile. I believe the book I have been sworn on. I have been to the prisoner's house since he has forbidden me; in hisabsence.
Q. Did he not forbid you the house, in consequence of the gross improprieties he had detected, between you and his wife? A. He said he was jealous of me, and his wife - I have known him about a year and a half.
COURT. Q. Were you at Bartholomew fair with the prisoner's wife? A. Yes; the prisoner was not there - I was there about three hours the first day, and about four hours the second - I do not recollect being there more than two days; we went afterwards to a large gin-shop, at the top of Shoe-lane - I do not know the sign; the prisoner did not know we were going there; his wife told him that week, or the next week, when she went to see him, where he was in possession for a Sheriff's officer; she told me so.
Q. Did she say he took it very much amiss? A. No; he was very satisfied.
JURY. Q. Do you know whether you went more than twice? A. I cannot swear - I might have gone three times.
COURT. Q. If you did go the third day, how long were you with her? A. I cannot say that I did go that day; if I knew the truth, I would tell you.
ANN WOODS. I live at No. 2, Mark's-place. On the 19th of November I heard a scuffle outside my door - I took a candle to see what was the matter, and saw Grover stabbing at Mr. Williams, and heard him say, "D-n you, I will kill you, I will, by G-d" - I then shut my door.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not appear to be in a violent rage? A. He did not appear so to me; he did not seem in the least passion - I have known Grover and his wife four years; Williams has lived in the court about half a year.
Q. Until he came into the court, did they not live very comfortable together? A. I have heard his wife say, she could not live with him - I never quarrelled with Grover.
COURT. Q. You understood they did not live very happily before he came? A. She told me they did not, I cannot say further; they have not lived worse together since, that I know of - I heard a great quarrelling between them, on the night before.
Q. When was it that the wife told you they did not live well together? A. When she was in the court one day - I cannot say how long before; it was repeatedly, and before Bartholomew fair.
JOHN JONES. I am a surgeon. I went on the 19th of November, and found the prosecutor stabbed in the upper surface of the arm; it is not severe, nor dangerous; it was in the fleshy part of the arm.
MR. PHILLIPS to JOHN WILLIAMS. Q. Were you looking at the execution in the Old Bailey, on Thursday, the 23d of November? A. No, Sir, I was not.
Q. Were you in the Old Bailey, near the execution, upon that morning? A. No, Sir, I was not.
Q. Then you was not there with the prisoner's wife, while the prisoner was laying in prison? A. I was not, before God and my country.
ABRAHAM COLEY. I am a constable. I went to take up the prisoner, who lived on the other side of the court; he was in an agitated state; he said Williams had destroyed his peace of mind, and his wife was taking away her clothes, and was going to live with Mr. Williams; he pulled out the drawers, and showed me that they were empty - I asked if he was aware he had wounded him in that dreadful manner; he said No, he was so agitated he was not aware that he had wounded him, if he had, he could have made his escape.
Q. What, you thought Williams' life in danger? A. Yes - I saw the wound; it appeared to me to be a dreadful one; the prisoner went with me without any resistance.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not appear in great agitation of mind, and distress? A. Yes; he showed me some empty drawers.
Prisoner's Defence. My Lord and Gentlemen of the Jury - Overwhelmed as I am at the deplorable situation in which I now stand before you, will, I am fearful, preclude the possibility of my revealing to you what I have suffered, both in body and mind, in consequence of the treatment I have experienced from the prosecutor, whose conduct towards me, I trust I shall be able to convince you, has beenmost infamous. Gentlemen, I have invariably had the most ardent affection for my wife, to whom I have been married twelve years and upwards; we have always lived most happily together, until the month of September last, when the prosecutor, by some means or other, began to cultivate her acquaintance; of which I received, from time to time, the most decisive and undoubted proofs; when, of course, that happiness which I had so many years enjoyed in her society, I was deprived of, which affected my spirits to that degree, that I have at times been in a state of distraction; I have, on many occasions, remonstrated with the prosecutor, in consequence of the improper behaviour towards me - indeed, I even found him with my wife, in my own house, which confirmed the reports of my neighbours, as to the criminal intercourse which had been going on for three months between them, - and I feel assured there are several persons in Court who will hear testimony to what I have asserted, and, above all, satisfy you that I have always been considered an honest, sober, diligent, and humane man. I am aware, Gentlemen, I cautioned the prosecutor on one occasion, never to presume to make any overtures to my wife, as he had done before, for the purpose of inducing her to elope with him - otherwise, I held out threats of inflicting that punishment upon him which he richly deserved; but I most solemnly deny that I had any other object in view at the time than that of intimidating or deterring him from visiting my house, and thereby preventing him taking the advantage of my absence from home. At the time, or a few minutes before I was accused of having struck at the prosecutor, I had been taking some refreshment, and the knife I used on the occasion, I could not have been aware I had in my hand, owing to the aggravating conduct of the prosecutor, which so much irritated my feelings as to cause me to strike him, when I must have been in a state of irritation, which was caused by the prosecutor placing himself in my way, laughing and jeering at me, or I could not have committed such a henious offence as that for which I now stand at this bar to answer, and which, should the prosecutor's expectations be realized, will banish me from my native country, or consign me to an ignominious death. Gentlemen, I can only add, that, upon your taking all the previous circumstances to which I have adverted, into your serious consideration, coupled with the evidence which you will hear in support of it, I feel confident that you will say that the injury which the prosecutor has received was not premeditated.
ABRAHAM COLEY re-examined. I did not see the knife - I searched for it.
JOHN WILLIAMS. I do not know what became of the knife.
JURY. Q. Had you spoken to the prisoner? A. No.
THOMAS KEARSLEY. I am a master shoemaker, and live at No. 9, Plumbtree-court, Shoe-lane. I have known Williams for years. I was at the Red Lion public-house, during Bartholomew fair - I know the prisoner's wife now, but not till then. I saw her on the Tuesday evening of the fair; the prosecutor heard her called by the name of Mrs. Williams - he was by her side; my wife heard it.
Q. Did he call her Mrs. Williams? A. Yes, as conversation happened; I cannot be mistaken about this: we were sitting together, drinking wine and water; I drank her health.
COURT. Q. Had you ever known her before? A. No. - I had every reason to believe, from their conduct, that she was Mrs. Williams - they behaved as man and wife ought to behave.
- KEARSLEY. I am the wife of the last witness. I was at the Red-lion public-house on the Tuesday, and saw a woman, in the prosecutor's presence, whom I now know to be the prisoner's wife - she went by the name of Mrs. Williams; Mrs. Bosley and Mrs. Renshaw were there.
ELEANOR BORELEY. I am married - my husband is an appraiser. I saw the prosecutor at the Red Lion during Bartholomew fair; he had Mrs. Grover with him, as I have since understood; she went by the name of Mrs. Williams; I drank her health as such; she appeared as the prosecutor's wife - he was sitting by her side, and could not have failed to have heard it.
MARY RENSHAW. I am the wife of George Renshaw, a tailor. I was in the Red Lion, Shoe-lane, during Bartholomew fair; there was a woman there with the prosecutor - I do not know who she was - she went by the name of Mrs. Williams; I drank her health as such, and wished her health and happiness in this world; she appeared as his wife; he sat close by her.
WILLIAM WILLIAMS. I am in the employ of the Sheriff of Middlesex. I was at the execution in November last; at that time Grover was in prison; I saw the prosecutor there with Mrs. Grover; I knew them both before, and could not be mistaken in the person of either of them; my wife was with me.
ANN WILLIAMS. I am the wife of the last witness, and was at the last execution at the Old Bailey, in November, and saw the prosecutor there with Mrs. Grover; she was standing at Mr. Richardson's door, No. 15, and the prosecutor standing by her side; I could not be mistaken in her person.
JURY. Q. Did you make any remark? A. Yes; I said what a shocking thing it was for her to look at such a thing; when she did not know how soon her husband might be in the same situation - I did not speak to her - my husband would not allow me.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271206-228]
229. SAMUEL LE COUNT, JOHN OWEN, and WILLIAM WARREN, were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 10 yards of woollen cloth, value 3l., the goods of James Harris.
JAMES BUTLER RIDGWAY. I am shopman to Mr. James Harris, a linen-draper, of Fore-street. On the evening of the 19th of November, an opposite neighbour sent a young man to say there were three suspicious characters about our door - I took my hat and went to the opposite side of the way, and saw the three prisoners all go to the shop - they each of them took hold of a piece of woollen cloth and moved it - they then stood about three quarters of an hour, watching the movements of the persons in the shop; I then saw Warren go in, and take hold of the cloth; I ran over, and he dropped it close to the door; I seized him and Le Count, and took them into the shop; the officer brought in the other; when Warren took the cloth the other two were at the window, and holding up their fingers, as signals.
JAMES VINT. I am a constable. I saw the three prisoners at the shop; I went down Moor-lane, and watched them; I saw them at the window, and they went several times to the door, and went away and whispered, and then they went to the door again; I then saw Ridgway run over; there were some persons going by at the time, and I did not see the cloth taken, but I saw Owen running, and I took him - I had watched them for about ten minutes.
JOHN CARLISLE. I was watching the prisoners for about half an hour - I saw one and another go to the door, but there were people passing, and I did not see the cloth moved.
LE COUNT's Defence. I was waiting for my father.
OWEN's Defence. I was looking in at the window, and then walked away.
WARREN's Defence. I was looking into the shop, and this gentleman came and struck me, and threw me into the shop.
Owen and Warren received good characters.
LECOUNT - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
OWEN - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Confined Six Months.
WARREN - GUILTY. Aged 15.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18271206-229]
230. JAMES GOLDSWORTH was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of May, 14 sovereigns, 1 sixpence, 4d. in copper monies, and one 10l. Bank-note, the property of Joseph Lee, his master.
JOSEPH LEE. I am a drover. The prisoner was in my service, and was in the habit of receiving and paying money for me. On the 25th of May I gave him a 10l. note, fourteen sovereigns, a shilling, a sixpence, and 4d. to go to Smithfield to pay to Mr. Hill, for eighteen sheep and four lambs - he was to take them to Mr. George Warner, in Bermondsey-street, who is my employer; the prisoner was to have met me at Romford fair on the following Wednesday, but I did not see him again till the 1st of September, when he was in Mile-end-road; I ran after him, but he escaped - he was taken again on the 5th of November.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Where did you give him the money? A. At the corner of Dean-street. Tooley-street, but I opened it again at the corner of Thames-street, to see that it was all right - he was to pay it for two different salesmen - it is entered in a book, which is not here; I went immediately to Romford, and staid there till Wednesday - whether he went to my house or not I cannot tell.
CHARLES HILL. The prisoner did not pay me any money on the 22d of May, nor at any time afterwards; I expected the money.
SARAH LEE. I am the wife of Joseph Lee. My husband came home to go to Romford; he told me he had sent the prisoner for the sheep - I saw him afterwards, three different times, in a different dress, and a different dog with him; I tried to get an officer, but could not till the third time, when I had him taken.
THOMAS STIMPSON. I am headborough of Mile-end Old Town - I received the prisoner in charge - he told me he had received the money and lost it out of his pocket, but he could not tell how - I found nothing on him.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined Two Months, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18271206-230]
231. JOHN SMITH and HENRY BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 2d of November, 5 pairs of boots, value 4l. 6s., and 2 boots, value 14s., the goods of James Purcell.
JAMES PURCELL. I am a boot-maker; I have a shop in John-street, Minories, but I do not live there, On the 2d of November, I went to New-square, to get my tea, leaving my son in the shop, and all my boots safe; in about six minutes, my son came and said the shop was robbed; I went there, and as I opened the door, I found one pair of boots under my feet. I went and gave information, and Smith was brought in at the time with these boots, which are mine.
RICHARD BUSH SKILLERN. I have two odd boots, and one pair of top-boots, which I found on Smith, about six o'clock in the evening, on the 2d of November, in Wentworth-street, Whitechapel, about half a mile from the prosecutor's shop. I saw nothing of Brown at that time - I believe two Lambeth-street officers took him.
RICHARD PURCELL. I am the son of James Purcell; I am nine years old; my father left me in the shop while he went to get his tea; the two prisoners came to the shop; Smith said he wanted a boot-lace; I stooped to get one, and on lifting my head up, I saw Smith take the boots off the peggs by the wall, and give them to Brown, who stood near the door; they then ran away by the window - I had not seen them before, but I am certain of their persons.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Have you not said that Brown was outside the shop? A. Yes; he went outside the door again, and then the other reached him the boots - it was about five o'clock - it was dark; they were there about ten minutes. I am sure I cannot be mistaken in them. I do not know how many pairs of boots Smith took.(Property produced and sworn to.)
SMITH's Defence. I was going along Whitechapel, and a person gave me the boots to pawn - I did not know they were stolen; I was going into a pawnbroker's shop with them, and the officer took me.
BROWN's Defence. I am quite innocent - I never saw Smith before.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
BROWN - NOT GUILTY.

232. MARY ANN STOREY was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of November, two pillows, value 3s., and two pillow-cases, value 1s., the goods of Mark Beahan.
MARK BEAHAN. I live in Brown's-buildings, Princes-street, and am a clothes-dealer. I let the prisoner a furnished lodging on the 24th of October, and among the furniture were these two pillows, and pillow-cases; the first week the prisoner was there, I gave her notice to quit, but she staid three days longer than the week. I did not miss these things till she had left; there were other articles in the room - she is a soldier's wife in a Scottish reginent.
CHARLES STUBBING. I am shopman to Mr. Matthews, a pawnbroker, of the Minories. I took in these two pillows on the 8th of November, I believe, of the prisoner - this is the duplicate I gave for them.
JAMES ROBERTS. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and found on her this duplicate, and the key of the door.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. The things were pawned with my landlady's consent, and she had 1s. 6d. of the money.
GUILTY. Aged 19.

233. WILLIAM NASH was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of November, 12 sovereigns; five half sovereigns; 1 warrant, for payment of and value 6l. 3s. 8d., and four 5l. Bank notes, the property of Thomas Chickley Tiltman, from his person.
THOMAS CHICKLEY TILTMAN. I am a fruiterer, of Covent-garden and Newgate-markets. On Lord Mayor's day, I was at the end of Fleet-market, in Fleet-street; between three and four o'clock in the afternoon; I wanted to get through the crowd, but could not; I was trying to press through, and all at once the crowd left me - I put my hand to my pocket, and missed all my money. I missed four 5l. Bank notes, a cheque for 6l. 3s. 8d., and I believe twelve sovereigns, and five half sovereigns, which had been all in one pocket. I turned sharp round, and saw the prisoner, I collared him, and said "You have robbed me;" he struck at me, and knocked me away from holding him - he struck at me four or five times afterwards, but I received only one blow - I got a black eye. I still held him, and called for assistance; a person caught hold of one of his hands, and he said I was not robbed, for I had got my money in my pocket - he was trying to put his hand into my pocket again - I caught hold of his hand, told him to open it, and I took the notes from his hand - the cheque was wrapped in them; I said, "Now give me my sovereigns" - he said he had not got them - I put my hand to his waistcoat-pocket, found something rattle, and said, "You have;" he then put his hand into his pocket, and gave me eleven sovereigns and five half-sovereigns.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was he searched afterwards? A. Yes, and a sovereign and some silver found on him; I had lost some papers, which were not found - the prisoner had not time to pick the money up - he did not say he had done so.
SIMEON ODWELL. I am a publican. Tiltman called out, "For God's sake, assist me; I have lost all my money;" I turned round, and saw the prisoner beating him all over his face - the blood ran from his mouth - I caught him by the hand and collar; the money was found on him; he took the gold from his pocket himself.
HENRY BOLTON. I took the prisoner to the watch-house; I found one sovereign and 14s. 6d. on him - I returned him the silver.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, declaring his innocence, but not accounting for his possession of the money.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18271206-233]
234. MARY CONNOLLY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of December, 1 sovereign, the money of Thomas Carpenter.
ELIZA CARPENTER. I am the wife of Thomas Carpenter, who belongs to the ship Ramilies. On the 5th of December I came up from Queenborough, to go to Somerset-house, to receive his half-pay; I received three sovereigns, and 5s. 4d. I left Somerset-house about twelve o'clock, and went to Billingsgate, but was too late for the boat; as I returned the prisoner stood on the stairs, and said, if I would give her a glass of any thing to drink she would show me a boat that was going away - I went into a house, and gave her a glass of gin; I did not sit down; we came out; she said, "I can't find any boat;" I said, "Very well, good night." I was going to Fleet-street; I went into the Three Cranes public-house, to get a pint of beer, and there the prisoner came in with some friends; she asked me for drink, and I drank with her; I then said I would pay for some more; she then asked how much I had got, and how much I had laid out; she was at the bar; I went into a side room, took out my money, and put it down on the table - I had two sovereigns, and nearly 1l. in silver; she came in behind me, and took a sovereign off the table; she ran outside the little room, came in again in about five minutes, and put down a sixpence: I said nothing to her - I do not think she left the house - I said, "That is not the money you took;" she abused me very much, and I said,"You had better give me the money back again, or I shall see further into it." I gave her in charge.
RICHARD GEORGE STATHAM. I am a constable. I took the prisoner in charge, and said, "Mary, I must search you;" she used some dreadful language, and stripped herself quite naked; she flew at a man in the tap-room, and beat him; she was made to put her clothes on again, and in going along, from inside her portering-hat, this sovereign slipped down.
Prisoner's Defence. This woman came to me, and treated me; we were both drunk; the officer said there was a sixpence in my hat - I said it was not mine - he told the Alderman it was a sovereign - it is likely he put it in.
GUILTY. Aged 30.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18271206-234]
235. JAMES HARRIS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
EDWARD CURETON. I am a wholesale-ironmonger, and live in Bread-street, Cheapside. Mr. Williams, an ironmonger, of the Commercial-road, is a customer of mine. - On the 10th of November, about half-past eight o'clock, the prisoner, who was a stranger, came to me - he produced a written order, which he said he brought from Mr. John Williams; I sent for an officer, and had him placed outside the door, to take the prisoner as he went out. I delivered him two dozens of sixteen-inch rasps - he said they were to go on board a ship which would be in the river the next tide; here is the order - (read.)
Mr. Curter, please send the following order by barer: - six dozens of fifteen-inch horse rasps, the best you have; if he cannot bring them all, send four dozens, as they are wanted immediately, to complete an order to go on bord of ship, and send the others by the Poplar errand-cart, to-day if you please.
J. DOYLE, for J. WILLIAMS.
JOHN WILLIS. I am a constable, and took the prisoner as he came out of the house.
JOHN DOYLE. I am foreman to Mr. John Williams. - This order is not my writing, nor Mr. Williams'. I know the prisoner, but never sent him with this order; he lived with us as errand-boy two years ago, and knew where we sent for articles.
Prisoner. The order was given to me - I do not know who wrote it.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
[trialAccount t18271206-235]

JOSEPH SHEFFIELD. I am in partnership with Charles, Henry, and Edward Sheffield, of Wellington-place, Lime-house. This order purports to be from us, but we gave none such. The prisoner was in our employ about two years ago.

SAMUEL THOMAS DAVIS. I am a coachmaster. ELIZA PAINE. 241. EDWIN PARTRIDGE was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of November, 4 pairs of stockings, value 5s., the goods of Thomas Slater.
ANN HANNAH ATKINSON. I am in the employ of Thomas Slater, a haberdasher, of Pitfield-street, Hoxton: the prisoner came there on the 30th of November - he asked for some cotton - I served him - he said it was too coarse - I then showed him another ball, which he said would do; he paid me, and went to the shop door - he turned and snatched a parcel of stockings out of the window; he ran away - I called out, but he made his escape then, and was taken the following evening; I am quite sure he is the same boy - he dropped the stockings in the street.
MARY ARNOLD. I was sitting at tea - I heard the cry Stop thief! I ran out, and picked up one pair of stockings four or five doors off - I gave them to Mr. Slater - I did not see the prisoner near the house.
THOMAS SLATER. On the evening of the 30th of November I was in the room, and heard the alarm - I did not see the transaction, but I know the stockings.
WILLIAM LATHAM. I took the prisoner on Saturday, the 1st of December - I came down the street just after the robbery had been committed, and saw Atkinson against the door, almost fainting; she gave me a description of the prisoner, and I took him the evening following.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
