242. BENJAMIN SALMON was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of November, 1 watch, value 6l.; 1 watch-chain, value 2l.; 1 seal, value 1l., and 1 watch-key, value 10s., the goods of John Loney.
JOHN LONEY. I am carpenter of a ship. On the 2d of November my vessel was lying in the river - I was in the Borough-road at seven o'clock that evening, and stopped to see a man taken to the watch-house; I got shoved down twice - it was done designedly - I got up, and walked away - I looked to see what time it was, and my watch was gone; there were a great many people about - I cannot say whether the prisoner was there.
FREDERICK GROSSMITH. I am an officer. On the 2dof November, about half-past eight o'clock in the evening, I was turning into Goulston-street, Whitechapel, and saw the prisoner - I asked what he wanted there - he said nothing - I knew he had come from the Borough, and said, "I think you have something to fence," meaning to sell - he said, No - I searched him, and found this watch in his hand, which he had behind him - he said he had found it at first, and afterwards said, "You know very well where I got it: you had better take it, and let me go- it will pay you better;" I took him to the watch-house.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I found it in the Kent-road.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18271206-242]
243. JOHN BURGESS and JOSEPH HUGHES were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of December, 1 hat, value 4s., the goods of James Watman Philpott.
JAMES WATMAN PHILPOTT. I am a hatter, and live at Shadwell. At twelve o'clock on Saturday night, I saw the prisoner talking together at my door: Burgess is in my employ, and was putting up the shutters - I saw Hughes go by - I went out, as if I was going away, and saw Hughes go and speak to Burgess, who gave him a hat - he and another boy walked away with it - I went between them, and tried to lay hold of both; they separated - I pursued Hughes, who dropped the hat, and ran into the watchman's arms - Burgess has lived three years with me; I would take him again.
JOHN ROTHER. I am a watchman, and took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Burgess received a good character.
BURGESS - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.
HUGHES - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Six Months, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18271206-243]
Fifth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
244. JOHN STACK was indicted for feloniously assaulting William Giles, on the King's highway, on the 16th of November, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 10l.; 1 watch-chain, value 3l., and 2 seals, value 2l., his property.
MR. WILLIAM GILES. I am private secretary to Mr. Hoare, the banker, of Fleet-street. On the 16th of November, about half-past five o'clock in the evening, I was in Little Queen-street, going out of Great Queen-street into Holborn; the prisoner met me, and gave me two touches - I cannot call them blows; he then took my watch from me, and ran down Parker-street; I followed crying, "Stop thief! he has got my watch;" we raced for about one hundred and fifty yards, and he was taken by Mr. George - I was about fifteen or sixteen yards behind him then; there was an old box of some kind found on him, but I have never seen the watch since.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you ever say you were not certain whether the prisoner was the person? A. No; I told the Magistrates I could not swear to his visage, as I never saw that till he was taken; but I never lost sight of his person - I did not see him give it to any body.
COURT. Q. What sort of a blow was it? A. It was morely to make me give way, and then there was the snatch at the watch.
ROBERT GEORGE. I am a coach-builder, and live in Parker-street. I was standing at my door; I heard the cry of Stop thief! and saw the prisoner running; I sprang after him, and caught the tail of his coat; he said, "Pray let me go - it is only a row;" he then took up the tail of his coat, and ran about twenty yards further, when he cried Stop thief! himself; I pursued him again, and struck him - we both fell; I pushed him into a door-way, and he was taken.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see him searched? A. Yes; there was no watch found on him; he had no opportunity of parting with it, unless he dropped it - he did not throw it away - he ran with his hands before him.
SAMUEL FURZMAN. I am an officer. On the 16th of November, between five and six o'clock, I was sent for, and found the prisoner in Parker-street; I found a pair of seissors, a tobacco-box, and a stopper on him, but no watch. I took two lights, and searched from the place up to Queen-street, but I found no watch.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you make a diligent search all the way you was told the prisoner had run? A. Yes; I found some mud had been disturbed - it appeared as if it had been moved with fingers; I found nothing.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming from my sister's; I heard a cry, and saw two persons running; I saw two persons run against the prosecutor; I went down the street - there were three or four different turnings, and the thief must have run up there.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Of stealing from the person only.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18271206-244]
245. JAMES THOMPSON, WILLIAM HAWKINS, and RICHARD LETT, were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 1 looking-glass, value 4l., and 1 oil painting, value 4l., the goods of Carlo Cattaneo, in his dwelling-house.
JOSEPH NEALE. I live in Guildford-place, Bagniggewells, two doors from Mr. Cattaneo. On the 16th of November, about one o'clock in the day, I saw Thompson and Hawkins at his window, and Lett was trying to open his door - he was against the key-hole. I went home, and in about ten minutes I saw Thompson go by our house, with a looking-glass on his left shoulder; I said to my master's foreman, "That is stolen." I then went, and told Cattaneo, while Davison pursued. I met Hawkins coming out of the door with a painting - I saw him come out of the door; I tried to stop him - he shoved against my breast, and made off - I did not see Lett there.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You did not particularly notice the men at first? A. Yes; I did - I came up to their side - I am quite confident of them; Thompson had carroty hair and a fustian jacket - they were secured at the top of St. Helena-place.
JOHN DAVISON. I am a baker. I was at home; Neale came in to give an account of his bread; I was putting it down, and saw Thompson carrying the glass on his left shoulder; Lett went by; Neale asked if he should go and tell the prosecutor; I said, "Do, and I will follow them." I ran out, and before we got to the top of the street, I turned, and saw Hawkins behind, with the painting;Thompson and Lett went into Wilmington-place; we followed them to St. Helena-place; Hawkins went as far as Noble-street, and then set off running. I followed Thompson and Lett - they walked on faster, and I ran a little; Stop thief! was cried; I then came up, and took Thompson with the glass on his shoulder - Lett got away; I waited there a few minutes, when Cattaneo came up, and gave him in charge; Thompson said the lad who was with him gave him 6d. to take the glass.
Cross-examined. Q. Thompson called Stop thief! when Lett ran away? A. I did not hear it; he might.
WILLIAM COLLINS. I am a cabinet-maker, and live in Margaret-street, a few doors from the prosecutor - I saw Hawkins running down Noble-street, with the painting - I pursued, and took him in the cellar of an unfinished house.
JOHN SHEPHERD. I live in Union-street, with Mr. Crosby. I was serving customers with beer, at the corner of Wilmington-place - Thompson went by with the glass on his shoulder; Hawkins came by soon after, with a picture, and in a moment Cattaneo came round the corner, calling Stop thief! - I pursued up Noble-street, and saw Hawkins throw the painting down - I took it up, gave it to a woman, and still pursued to Amwell-street, where I saw Collins, and took Hawkins in an area; he said, "What do you want with me?" I said, "You have robbed Mr. Cattaneo's shop of some glass;" he said, "No - I have come here for a certain purpose." and while Collins was gone for a constable, Hawkins knocked me down, and kicked me about; my nose has not been right since; he said, "If I had a knife, I would stick it into your bl-dy heart;" some people about said, I was no officer, and had no right to take him - I said I would hold him - one Blatch assisted me in carrying him along; he threatened to stick a knife into Blatch's heart also; his clothes were buttoned up, when I found him in the area.
GEORGE WADDINGTON. I am an officer. Hawkins and Thompson were brought to the office, and a description of Lett was left - I went to Saffron-hill, and took him in about an hour and a half, and he was identified.
SAMUEL MOUNT STEPHEN. I am a constable of Clerkenwell. I met Hawkins in custody; he was resisting very much - I took charge of him.
CARLO CATTANEO. I live in Guildford-place, Spafields. I do not know the name of the parish - I keep a looking-glass shop - I was up stairs, and my door shut - I went down for some water, and saw the door open - I shut it, and found the lock strained; it would not fasten - I went up, and heard a cry of thieves, came down, ran out, and saw the witness with the glass - I saw Thompson, and collared him, and took him to Hatton-garden.(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMPSON'S Defence. I have worked for a woman named Harding. On the 6th of November she bought some things at a sale, in Cloak-lane, and on the 7th I went to fetch them; they were not ready - I went back to Union-street, and met a man with a glass; he said, "Take this glass, and carry it, my master will satisfy you;" I called Stop thief! after the man who gave it me.
HAWKINS' Defence. I went down the area for a necessary purpose - I did strike the witnesses, as I was aggravated.
LETT'S Defence. I was taken on Saffron-hill, but was not near the place.
THOMPSON - GUILTY. Aged 19.
HAWKINS - GUILTY. Aged 18.
LETT - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Of stealing, but not in the dwelling-house.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18271206-245]
246. MICHAEL RUSSELL was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of December, at St. Clement Danes, 25 yards of woollen cloth, value 10l., the goods of Thomas Bennett, in his dwelling-house.
THOMAS BENNETT. I live at No. 14, White Horseyard, Drury-lane, in the parish of St. Clement Danes, and am a woollen-draper. About one o'clock yesterday, I left my wife in the shop, and went to dinner; my wife called me up - I came, found the prisoner in the shop, and said to him, "How came you to come into a shop at this time of day, you seem not to want victuals, and have a good coat on your back;" he said, "I did it through distress" - I said, "Why don't you apply to the parish?" he made no answer - I had left this cloth on the counter, about three yards from the window, when I went down; I found it laying nearer to the window, but on the counter - I had not been absent five minutes; it might be about a yard from where it was before: after I told him about applying to the parish, he said he was an orphan, and in distress; the cloth contains twenty-five yards, and the lowest value is 10l.
MARY BENNETT. I am the prosecutor's wife. I was in the shop, but had not noticed the cloth, before I saw it in the prisoner's hands - I was sitting reading the newspaper, and the first thing I saw, was the prisoner turning from the counter, with the cloth in his arms; it was off the counter; he was turning from the counter with it; I did not speak for a moment, and he got to the door - I then said, Halloo! and he turned to the counter, and said, "I have brought this from Mr. Hume, for sale" - I paused for a moment, and said, "From who?" he said, "From Mr. Hume" - I said, "No, you have taken it off this counter;" he said, "Me! No, I have a young man outside, who saw me bring it" - I said, "You have not, for here is our mark on it" - I then knocked for my husband - I think I have seen the prisoner before, but not to know him.
Prisoner. Q. How long before had you seen the cloth? A. I had not noticed it, till it was in your arms - I did not get to the door to look for the young man; you put the cloth on the counter yourself.
JOHN MARSHALL. I am a constable of St. Clement Danes. I was sent for, and took the prisoner in charge with the property.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. In passing the corner of the court, a man came to me, and said, "Take this to the second shop, and tell them it comes from Mr. Hume, and he wants to sell it" - I was not certain whether that was the shop, and came back to look if Mr. Bennett's name was on the door; the woman said, it was hers - I said,"A young man at the corner gave it me to sell" - I told her to go to the door, to look at him, which she did.
MRS. BENNETT. I did not go to the door at all.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 16.
[trialAccount t18271206-246]
247. MARIA SMITH was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Shans, at St. Matthew, Bethnal-green, on the 17th of November, and stealing, 2 pelisses, value 8l.; 4 curtains, 4l.; 6 dresses, value 6l.; 1 shawl, value 2l.; 3 table-cloths, value 15s.; 10 yards of carpet, value 10s.; 2 pairs of sheets, value 12s.; 3 pillow-cases, value 4s.; 8 handkerchiefs, value 8s.; 3 caps, value 10s.; 2 rings, value 10s.; 1 pin, value 4s.: 2 brooches, value 10s.; 1 clasp, value 1s.; 1 necklace, value 2s.; 1 time-piece, value 10s., and 10 sovereigns, his property.
MARY SHANS. I am the wife of William Shans; we live at No. 5, Broughton-place, Hackney-road, in the parish of St. Matthew, Bethnal-green. On the 17th of November I went out, about half-past five o'clock in the morning, to market - I keep a small fruit-shop, and my husband is a gentleman's servant - I locked the door, and had the key in my pocket; all the doors and windows were secure - I returned at a quarter before seven o'clock, and found the outer door closed, but the moment I put the key to it, I found it was open - I went in, and found an inner door quite open, which I had left locked, and had the key in my pocket; both doors must have been opened with picklock keys - I was afraid to go in, but my husband's sister went in first - I stood at the outer door; she ran up stairs, and when I went in, I missed all the articles stated in the indictment, and several other things, which I cannot recollect - I value my pelisses at 8l., and my dresses at 6l. - I lost ten sovereigns from a writing-desk, and a great many other articles - I have recovered none of them - I went to the grocer's-shop, next door, and gave an alarm - I know nothing of the prisoner; she was taken on the following Friday.
ANN HOLT. I live at Mr. Jones' No. 9, Broughton-place. On Saturday, the 17th of November, at half-past six o'clock in the morning, I was going out for my milk, I saw Shans' street door open, and two women coming out, with their aprons full of something; I saw two other women go in, within three minutes after - the prisoner was one of those I saw come out; she was the outside woman, and had her apron full; I did not know her before - I am quite sure she is one of them. I did not see the other two women come out - I did not give information.
JOHN HOLLETT. I am a watchman - my beat is in Hackney-road. In consequence of information, and a description of the person, I and Rigg took the prisoner, and put her in the watch-house; we took another woman with her - Mrs. Holt identified the prisoner at the watch-house. The prisoner said to me at the watch-house, when the other woman was gone, "If I had spoke my bl-y mind, you would not have let that girl go;" she also said Mrs. Holt was a false-swearing old b-t-h.
JOHN RIGG. I am a watchman. I had information of the robbery, and said I thought I should find the person - and on Friday night I and Hollett took the prisoner and another girl.
JAMES GLIBBERY. I am a constable of Bethnal-green. The prisoner was brought to me on Friday night, about half-past nine o'clock - I found nothing on her but the key of her lodging.
Prisoner's Defence. On Wednesday night I spoke to Rigg, near the Olive Branch public-house, and he said,"Let me have none of your noise to-night - you are very abusive when in liquor;" and on the Friday night following I went with a young woman to have some drink; he came over, held up his light, and took me to the watch-house - Holt said at first that I had a bundle, which she thought was was broken victuals.
ANN HOLT re-examined. It did not seem like broken victuals; it was bulky, and more like wearing-apparel than any thing else.
JAMES GLIBBERY re-examined. The prisoner lives about three quarters of a mile from the prosecutrix's; she gave me a false direction the first night, but next morning she gave me a right direction; I found the place, and searched it, and when I went in I found the bed was warm, though I had the key in my pocket.
LEAH REYNOLDS. I am a Leghorn trimming weaver, and live at No. 1, Shepherd's-court, Old Nichols-street, in the adjoining room to the prisoner. On the Friday night she came home very much in liquor, between ten and eleven o'clock - she asked me for a light, which I gave her; and at three in the morning, I was taken very ill, and called out,"Maria Smith, are you in bed?" she said Yes, and asked if she should get up; I said No: I laid awake till seven, when my husband went out, and she asked me what time it was - I went into her room at nine, and she asked me to lend her a few halfpence, to get a breakfast. This was last Friday three weeks.
Q. How long had she lived there? A. She had lived there about a fortnight before the robbery; a young man used to come backwards and forwards to her; he was there that Friday night; they occupied a ready furnished room, up one pair of stairs - I remember the officer searching that room; the young man had slept there the night before the officer came.
Q. How did he get in? A. I think he drew the staple of the door. The prisoner keeps good hours - I do not know how she gets her living; she comes home about eleven or ten o'clock; I do not know whether the young man is her husband - I do not know what he is called. I go to bed about half-past nine or ten o'clock; my husband is a cabinet-maker, and has worked for Mr. Wright nearly four years, and I work when I can get any.
Q. What makes you so particularly remember the night in question? A. Because she came to my door for a light - she has done so before. I get up about half-past eight o'clock - I did not get up that morning till near nine; the prisoner was not up when I went into her room; I spoke to her about three o'clock, about seven, and again at nine; I have been taken ill before, and called to her. There is no lock to our street door, only a small latch. I do not know how she or the young man get their bread.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
[trialAccount t18271206-247]
SIXTH DAY. WEDNESDAY, DECEMBER 12.
Fifth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
248. JAMES FOGGOTER was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Robert Matthew Imeson, on the 5th of November, at St. Leonard, Shoreditch, and stealing 124 lbs. of tobacco, value 30l.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 10s.; 1 medal, value 5s., and 300 penny-pieces, his property.
ROBERT MATTHEW IMESON. I am a tobacconist, and live in Shoreditch, in the parish of St. Leonard, Shoreditch, and rent the house. On the morning of the 6th of November I was called up by my young man, who informed me the place was broken open and robbed; I dressed myself, came down, and found a large hole through the brick wall, which divides my back manufactory from the dwelling-house; I had shut my private door myself the night before, and my young man shut the shop door; the persons had first come across my manufactory, which is now empty, then cut through eighteen inches of the brick-work, and got into the parlour, which is part of the dwelling-house, and then through the private door into the shop; no implements were found in the house; several casks of tobacco in the shop were partly emptied, and a quantity of copper was taken; also a silver Livery-medal, of the Cutlers' Company, and a pair of silver sugar-tongs from the table in the little parlour, adjoining the shop.
CHARLES HENRY ANDERSON. I was in the service of Mr. Imeson. On the morning of the 6th of November I went to open the shop, and found the tobacco trod about the shop; I immediately went to the shop door, and found it unfastened and open; I locked it with the key, inside the door, ran up stairs, and informed Mr. Imeson of it - there was a hole in the wall, which divided the parlour from the warehouse, where they made their entry - the hole was cut through the bricks, large enough for a full sized man to get through.
CURSTON HULSEBERG. I am a tobacco manufacturer. On the 5th of November I sent 120 lbs. of Maryland and Virginia tobacco, mixed together, to Mr. Imeson's - the colour of it was rather peculiar.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How much of this tobacco is in London? A. I do not know - it is common, but it depends entirely on the colour.
THOMAS GARTON. I am a constable. On the 6th of November, about nine o'clock in the morning, I went to Mr. Imeson's premises. I saw the hole in the wall - I could have got through it easily. On the 8th of November I went, with Waters and Brown, to a house in a small court, in Austin-street - there are but two houses in the court; I placed Waters in the yard, and I knocked at the door; no one came, and I sent Brown in at the window; he opened the door - he and I went up stairs, and found the prisoner in bed with a woman; I called Waters in, and, in a box, close by the bed, I found this tobacco, tied in an apron and this other parcel in a woman's cap; I then found a small portion of snuff in a paper, which I gave to Brown; I then saw Brown find these two crow-bars, near the fire-place; I found two dark lanterns. I then told the prisoner that I took him on suspicion of a robbery at Mr. Imeson's, in High-street, Shoreditch, of some tobacco and half-pence. I took him and the woman to the office. I then went, with Brown and Waters, to Mr. Imeson's, and examined the desk and cupboard, which were broken open, in the shop; I found the marks on the desk exactly fitted this bar, and some lime was on the desk - there is also some on the bar.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You never saw the prisoner near the prosecutor's premises? A. No; this was two days after - nobody else lives in the prosecutor's house - the bill was not found against the woman.
JAMES BROWN. I am an officer. I went to the house with Garton and Waters - Garton's statement is correct; I found this small parcel of snuff, wrapped in a bill of parcels of Mr. Imeson's - I found these skeleton keys and this dark-lantern there - I swear this bill of parcels was found at the prisoner's house - I have had it ever since.
Cross-examined. Q. Was this bill produced at the office at the first examination? A. It was; it was not read nor examined - the snuff was produced and looked at - the Magistrate might look at it at a distance - he did not take it off the table; it had been in my possession about two hours.
THOMAS WATERS. I am an officer. I went with Brown and Garton, on the 8th of November, to the prisoner's house, and while they were up stairs, I found in a cupboard in the lower room, a dark-lantern, and a bunch of skeleton keys; and on the mantle-shelf a phosphorus-box, some matches, and, in the corner of the room, I found this string, which I produced before the Magistrate, and Mr. Imeson stated it to be part of the string with which he had tied up a parcel the night before; I found these three little jars of tobacco in the lower room.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you call these two keys skeleton? A. No; but a great part of the bunch are skeleton.
PHILEMON HARVY. I am a pawnbroker. On the 6th of November the prisoner came to my shop, in company with a woman, whom I know, but cannot say whether they were acquainted before - they redeemed a quantity of articles, which came to above 1l., and paid for them in copper, tied in brown paper - I think they were pence and halfpence: I saw the woman at the office - she was called Esther Smith.
SARAH GADBURY. My husband is a bricklayer: we live in Old Castle-street; the house the prisoner was taken at is ours; the prisoner lodged there - they had two rooms - they were taken by the woman called Smith, in the name of Foggoter - nobody else lived there - there is but two rooms in the house - it is in a court in Leicester-street: the rent was generally paid by the woman; the prisoner paid it once.
THOMAS GARTON re-examined. Esther Smith is the woman against whom the bill was thrown out, and who was before the Magistrate when Harvy was there.
MR. IMESON. The paper this snuff is in, is an invoice of my writing, dated the 5th of November - I had not sent it out - it was in a parcel of tobacco and snuff, for a person named Potter, whose name is on it; the parcel was gone from the shop when I got up in the morning - this string was round the parcel, and from the peculiar manner in which we tie it, and having had occasion to use two strings of different descriptions, I would not swear, but have no doubt, these are the same strings.
Cross-examined. Q. Was this bill produced on the first examination? A. It was not looked at, to my recollection.
COURT. Q. What is the value of the tobacco and snuff missing? A. Between 30l. and 40l.; but I could not swear to it.
JURY. Q. Are you sure Potter's parcel had not been sent out? A. It had not - I saw it in the night of the 5th, at nine o'clock, when I went to supper.
Prisoner's Defence. At my first examination thatpaper was opened, and both the Magistrate and Mr. Imeson took a pinch of it - it is very strange Mr. Imeson should not have looked at the paper - it was never observed, and Mr. Imeson said there was nothing by which it could be identified.
MR. IMESON. I did take a pinch of it, but never looked at the paper.
JURY. Q. Are you certain it is the same paper which was produced? A. Yes; the invoice was in the parcel, folded up as an invoice.
JAMES BROWN. I found the snuff in this invoice - the examination was but short, and it was hardly looked at.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 24.
[trialAccount t18271206-248]
249. RICHARD HART was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, at St. James, Westminster, 2 sheets, value 12s.; 2 gowns, value 2l.; 9 shifts, value 20s.; 1 pelisse, value 2l.; 1 pair of stays, value 5s.; 1 coat, value 30s., and 2 table-cloths, value 1l., the goods of Samuel Mitchell, his master, in his dwelling-house.
SAMUEL MITCHELL. I am a publican, and live No. 22, Broad-street, Golden-square, in the parish of St. James, Westminster. The prisoner was in my employ, and had slept in my house for about six weeks. On the evening of the 4th of December, between five and six o'clock, he came to the bar and asked for a light, to go up stairs; I thought it was to go and clean himself, as usual; he took the light, went up stairs, and I never saw him afterwards; in about an hour I went up stairs to clean myself, and found my bed-room door open - I went in - my wife followed me, to give me some clean linen - she opened the drawer, and missed her gowns; I then looked with her, and we missed the sheets and some damask table-cloths - she then missed some shifts; the prisoner never came back; next day we got most of our things back; I think no one of them is worth 5l. - the pelisse cost six guineas, when new, but is not worth that now.
WILLIAM BROWN. I am apprentice to a butcher, of Tottenham-court-road. The prisoner came to my master's shop about nine o'clock on Tuesday night, the 4th of December, with a bundle; he called me out of the shop, and asked me if Phoebe Joyce was at home - she is my master's servant - he said he was her brother, and had come out of the country, and he would be obliged if I would give her that bundle; he pulled out a handful of silver from his pocket, and gave me 6d. for myself.
JOSEPH COTTON. I am a pawnbroker, and live at Somer's-town - I produce some table-cloths and sheets, pawned on the 4th of December, between eight and nine o'clock at night - I cannot exactly say the time.
Q. Are you sure it was not before six o'clock? A. I think it was about that time - I forgot myself before - the prisoner pawned them.
BENJAMIN WEBB. I am an officer. I heard of this robbery, and went to No. 9, Ogle-street, and found this coat at the house of a man named Hampton, who was at the office, but is not here; on the next morning I went to Tottenham-court-road, and got this bundle from Phoebe Joyce - it contains nine shifts, two gowns, a pelisse, a pair of stays, and several other articles; I left the shop about ten minutes, and saw the prisoner on the other side of the way - I knew him, went up, and took him - he went quietly with me; I asked what he could think of himself for robbing so good a master; he said he was very sorry, but he could not help it; I found on him two duplicates - one of Mr. Cotton's, and another, and three keys, one of which easily opened Mr. Mitchell's bed-room door.
PHOEBE JOYCE. I know the prisoner. Brown gave me this bundle; I gave it to the officer.
ROBERT YOUNG. I am a pawnbroker, and produce a pair of sheets, pawned by the prisoner, on the 4th of December, in the evening.
SAMUEL MITCHELL. My bed-room door was locked before; all this property is mine - it is worth 15l. or 16l. in all, and was all safe that day.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 18.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor, supposing it to be his first offence.
[trialAccount t18271206-249]
250. WILLIAM LONGMAN and THOMAS HOUGH were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Henry Whitaker, on the 9th of November, at St. Leonard, Shoreditch, and stealing 1 hat, value 15s.; 1 coat, value 15s.; 2 waistcoats, value 16s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 12s.; 10 handkerchiefs, value 12s., and 1 set of harness, value 7l., his property.
HENRY WHITAKER. I am ostler at the King's Head, Kingsland-road, in the parish of St. Leonard, Shoreditch. Mr. Milledge keeps that house; I rented a house of my master, adjoining the public-house. On the 9th of November my place was broken open - I had fastened it about nine o'clock over night - I was called out of bed between four and five in the morning, and found the door broken open; it appeared to have been kicked open, and the lock was forced off the door; I missed my coat, hat, two waistcoats, a pair of trousers, ten handkerchiefs, and a gentleman's harness, which was in the same room, under my care; I had seen them all safe the night before; I know nothing of the prisoners, and have found none of my property.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Is your house in the public-house yard? A. Yes; it is next to the public-house, but there is no entrance from it to the public-house - I knew Hough by his coming to the public-house - I have drank with him - he was not a particular friend - I had seen him before. After locking up my house at nine o'clock the night before, I went to the public-house, and had a pint or two of beer, and I slept at my master's that night, as I had not a bed at my house; I had never slept in my house.
COURT. Q. When did you begin to rent it? A. Two or three weeks before, but I had not got the things ready to go into it.
Prisoner LONGMAN. Q. Were we not drinking together the night before? A. Yes, we were.
JOSEPH ABDELL. I had slept in this house, which the prosecutor had hired of his master; there are four rooms in it; I was in the room over the one which was broken into; it is all let out in rooms - Whitaker hired the two bottom rooms - I pay my rent to Mr. Milledge. I went out about half-past eleven o'clock at night, to do some nightwork, and saw the two prisoners and Lock standing close against the gate; I had heard no noise or breaking then - I opened the outer door, went out, and pulled it too, close;I returned at four in the morning, went in to go to bed, and directly I shoved the outer door open, I found Whitaker's room door open - I then went up stairs, and found mine all safe; I called to a woman who lived in the right-hand room.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not you know that Hough and the prosecutor are very intimate friends? A. I have seen them drink together, and I have seen Hough about there; I have never seen him working in the yard.
WILLIAM NORTON. I am a watchman. On Friday morning, the 9th of November, about half-past twelve o'clock, I saw the two prisoners and Lock come out of the King's Head-yard; Hough had a bag under his arm, I had not the least idea of it being stolen; I thought they had been at work down there, as Abdell, the nightman, lives down there. In the morning, I received information from Whitaker, went to his room, and saw the pannels broken, and the lock off the door.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been a watchman? A. About a year and a half. I had no suspicion of them; I was in search of them for four days before I could find them - they were all three absent.
JOHN LOCK. I was taken up for this felony, on Saturday night, the 10th of November. I went up to the King's Head, public-house, to get a pint of beer, on Thursday night, the 8th November, and Whitaker offered me a glass of rum and shrub; when I had drank it, I was going home; but Longman said "Don't go yet, I will put 1l. into your pocket;" when the house was shut up, we all went out, and stopped against the gate; Longman and Hough then went down the yard; the outer door of the house, where Whitaker lives, was open; Longman and Hough put their feet against Whitaker's room door, and broke it open; Longman went in, and brought out a hat, a coat, several waistcoats, trousers, handkerchiefs, and some harness; he gave them to Hough, who put them into a sack; we all went out of the yard - it was about twelve o'clock, or half-past; we went to Constable-alley, and Longman put the property under a house; we then went to Darkhouse-lane, near London-bridge, and staid their till four o'clock; we then returned to Constable-alley for the parcel, and I took it to a man named James Pagett, who brought a sovereign to the Golden Heart public-house, in Phoenix-street, kept by Van, that morning; I got it changed, and we had some steaks, and beer - the landlord took the reckoning out of it; in the course of the day, Pagett paid some more, which was put on the table and divided. I fetched the steaks myself from a butcher's in Brick-lane, and the landlord dressed them; the property was sold to Pagett.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you come here in custody? A. Yes, from the House of Correction; this is the second time I have been in a Court - I was here before, but only as a witness. I was in trouble myself last Session, on suspicion of carrying a parcel - I was not tried; the gentleman did not come against me - I was at the bar.
Q. What did that parcel contain? A. Harness; I was never in custody on any other occasion. I did not break the door, nor carry the goods - I swear I did not break the door.
JURY. Q. Did you sell this property? A. No; I saw Pagett about selling it; he gave 2l. for it all; he asked Longman how much he wanted - he told him 2l.; he lives at No. 2, Hope-street, Spitalfields. I had 5s. out of the sovereign, and we had 5s. each afterwards, and the beef steaks and drink among us. I took the things to Pagett, between six and seven o'clock in the morning - Pagett is not here.
THOMAS WATERS. I am an officer. I took Lock at the Job's Castle public-house, near Shoreditch; I have been after Pagett several times - he has absconded
THOMAS VAN. I keep the Golden Heart public-house, Phoenix-street, which is not more than twenty yards from Hope-street; I remember Lock and the two prisoners being there on Friday morning, and Pagett came in while they were there. I changed a sovereign at the bar for Lock - I did not see from whom he received it - I heard nothing pass between them; they had some beef steaks, which Lock brought - I cooked them.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you seen Lock before? A. Yes; I knew Pagett - he was a near neighbour.
GEORGE SMITH. On the 9th of November I was called up, and told of the robbery. I went with Norton and Whitaker, and saw the door was broken open; the watchman told me what he had seen: I went in search, but nothing has been found; Pagett's house was searched on Tuesday afternoon.
HENRY WHITAKER re-examined. I took Hough into custody; he came into a public-house where I was sitting, and I charged him with this robbery - he said he had not done it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not say, "You had better come before a Magistrate, and clear yourself?" A. Yes- he said, "I am agreeable," and went. The witnesses stated what they have now, but Hough gave no account of himself.
COURT. Q. Do you hire these rooms of your master? A. Yes, to take care of some property, and I intend to live in them; I had been living in the public-house, and continued there till this house was fitted up.
Q. Had you the key of the outer door? A. Yes.
MR. BARRY. Q. Had not Mr. Milledge a room or two in the house, for his own use? A. No - he let it all in seperate tenements. There are four rooms - I have two, Abdell one, and a widow has one; the street door was open when I went out; I had closed my own door, and am positive that I locked it.
LONGMAN'S Defence. We were all drinking in the public-house that evening.
HOUGH'S Defence. Norton says I was carrying the sack; Lock says Longman carried it. I stated to Whitaker that I went down to the Dun Horse, public-house, where I had lodged a few nights.
THOMAS WATERS. He did not state that in my hearing.
Two witnesses gave Hough a good character.
LONGMAN - GUILTY - DEATH Aged 22.
HOUGH - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 19.
[trialAccount t18271206-250]
251. JOHN COLEMAN was indicted for feloniously assaulting Mary Ann Davis, on the 8th of December, putting her in fear, and taking from her person, and against her will, 1 handkerchief, value 5s.; 23 yards of lace, value 2l., and 6 yards of net, value 36s., the goods of William Davis.
SECOND COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Mirian Farrow.
MARY ANN DAVIS. I am the wife of William Davis. - On Saturday evening, the 8th of December, about half-past eight o'clock, I was walking up Tottenham-court-road, and near Southampton-court, the prisoner came from behind me, round my right side, in front of me, and snatched my bundle, containing this property, from me - he did not strike me, but merely pulled it; I tried to keep it, and he pulled it from me; he made off with it - it was the property of Mirian Farrow; I had it to make into a dress for her. I called Stop thief! ran, and caught him myself, without losing sight of him, in Southampton-court; I asked him where the bundle was - he said he did not know - he had not got it; he ran about fifty yards. The bundle has not been found.
Q. How could you lose sight of the bundle? A. I do not know what he did with it - I am quite positive of him.
WILLIAM PERKINS. I am a watchman. I was at my box, and heard a cry of Watch! I ran, and found the prosecutrix holding the prisoner by the collar; he shook, but said nothing. It is a very bad place - there is nothing but thieves there; there were several cads about - it is near the coach-stand. I have seen the prisoner in the coach-rank.
Prisoner's Defence. I heard a call of Stop thief! and came up; a young man ran past me, and in half a minute this young lady seized me.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Of stealing from the person only.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18271206-251]
252. WILLIAM STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of August, 10 flag-stones, value 9s., the goods of the Commissioners for paving and lighting the parish of St. James, Westminster .
SECOND COUNT, stating it to belong to Joseph Stevenson and others.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
MARY KINSLEY. In August last I lodged in the prisoner's house, No. 22, Queen-street, Golden-square. On the evening of the 2d of August, about seven o'clock, I saw the prisoner take three large flags out of the street, when the men were gone home; he put them into the yard, under my bed-room window - next morning I heard a noise, and saw him take seven others, and put them with the other three. I gave information at the office, and he was taken.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. And was he not bailed? A. I believe he was - I had lodged six or seven months with him; he never gave me notice to quit his premises; he employed a broker to distrain on me after this charge, and he held his fist up at me at the door, and said, he would send his fist through me, because I told about these stones; he did not distrain before this business - I told the gentlemen of it directly it was done, and they told me to go to Mr. Johnson's - I swear he did not distrain before I made the charge - I went and told the gentlemen about this, and then he put the broker in - I did not owe him a farthing; he never employed a broker but once, and that was after I gave the information.
Q. Was he not obliged to employ a constable to protect himself? A. I do not know - I never saw a constable; my six children were turned into the street - I was outside in the street - I did not break his window-shutters down, or any thing of the sort; my husband is not here; my daughter is here, who saw the stones taken - I went to make the complaint to an officer in the course of the same day, as he took them - I met the officer in the street, and he told me to go to Mr. Winkfield's - I know Ann Byrne - I never told her the poor Irishman would suffer for the stones which were brought into his yard, nor any body else - nor did I ever say to her, "What a nice lot of stones there is in that yard - the poor Irishmen will suffer for this."
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Had you any dispute with the prisoner about rent, or any thing else, till after you told about the stones? A. No - I went to Johnson that day, and by his direction, went to Marlborough-street, and was taken over the way to Mr. Winkfield's office.
MARTIN FARRELL. I am superintendant of the paving of St. James', Westminster. Mr. Joseph Stevenson is one of the paving committee - Queen-street was being paved in August last, and many loose stones were there - I saw Kinsley, and in consequence of what she said, I went to the prisoner's house, and in his back-yard, found eight Yorkshire, and two Berwick stones - I did not see the prisoner there - I saw him at the office - I could not swear to the stones; what passed at the office, was not taken down, I am certain; the prisoner stated there, that the workmen had given him these stones; they had no right to do so; they were of the same quantity and description as those in the street.
Q. About what value are they? A. Nine shillings; it is not an open yard; there are several lodgers there - I believe many of them are Irish.
Q. Had not the prisoner gone personally to the office to object to something about the paving rate? A. Yes, and there this charge was made; it was the latter end of July I saw Kinsley, and on the 2d of August I saw the stones.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Had he been summoned for the rate? A. Yes, by the collector, and attended, to show cause against it; he is a slater.
MR. PHILLIPS called -
ANN BYRNE. I know Kinsley well; she told me there was a nice lot of stones in the yard, and the poor Irishman would be a sufferer for it - I will be on my oath to that - I live in the house; there are six or eight families there: this was the morning after the stones were brought in - I saw a labouring man bring some in, and there were some there before.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Were stones often brought there? A. There were some before - I cannot say when they were brought in - the stones were brought in three weeks or a month before the words occurred between the prisoner and Kinsley - I have lived there above twelve months - these stones were brought from his other house, at Westminster - I cannot say where the last were brought from - it was not the prisoner who brought them in - he was taken up three weeks or a month afterwards - the man who brought them never called for them - the broker came in before the last stones were brought - I live in the kitchen - a knock came to the door, and I answered it when the stones came.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. She made no complaint for three weeks after they were brought? A. No; they quarrelled on Tuesday - the broker came in on Wednesday - and next day she went to the office.
CHARLES READER. I live with Mr. Brooks, next door to the prisoner; he is a builder; I was sweeping the door one evening, and saw some men taking these stones in: I did not see the prisoner; I did not see who opened the door.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How many stones were there? A. I did not notice; I saw no cart; the street was being repaired - it was between six and seven o'clock in the evening.
JOHN HARGRAVE. I am a carman. I brought some old paving stones from the prisoner's old house, Johnson-street, Westminster, and put them into his back yard.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. When was that? A. I think in February.
WILLIAM SKATE. I am a house-agent, and live in Castle-street, Leicester-square. I know Kinsley, and would not believe her on her oath.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you ever see her examined in a Court? A. No; I heard her examined at Marlborough-street, but not on this business - I knew her before that - she swore at the office that I had struck her - I was the broker, and she was not present at the time - it was on the 25th of July that I levied.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you treat her harshly? A. No; she was not present; I never turned her into the street.
MARY KINSLEY re-examined The stones were brought the latter end of July, at half-past seven o'clock in the evening, and five in the afternoon; Byrne lives in the kitchen, and said nothing to her about the poor Irishman suffering - I gave information the next day, but it was a week or a fortnight before they took any notice of it, and then I went to Marlborough-street.
ANN DIXON. My husband is a retired government officer: I lived in the prisoner's house two years; Kinsley said to me, "There is a rare lot of stones brought in, and the poor Irishmen will suffer." I did not wish to come forward this morning, but, hearing her state what she has, I thought it my duty.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How came you here? A. Merely from curiosity - Byrne was not with me when I heard this - I was in Kinsley's back kitchen - I do not visit such creatures as her; but she called me in, and told me that.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271206-252]
253. ELEANOR HEDLEY was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Hinxman, on the 1st of December, and stealing 1 counterpane, value 5s.; 1 table-cloth, value 1s.; 4 towels, value 2s.; 12 pairs of stockings, value 12s., and 1 handkerchief, value 1s., his property.
ANN HINXMAN. I am the wife of John Hinxman: his country residence is at Golder's-green, Hendon - the prisoner had been my servant - on the 1st of December I was in London - she had then left me about ten days; these articles are all mine, and were at my house at Hendon.
HENRY KINNETT. I am in the service of Mr. Hinxman. I saw this property hanging at their laundry at Hendon, to dry, on the 1st of December, and at half-past eight o'clock in the evening, it was taken in, and put into baskets - the laundry is not a part of the dwelling-house - it is at some distance in the yard; the yard is between the dwelling-house and the laundry; I slept that night in a room over the laundry - I went to bed at half-past eight o'clock; there is a double sash window to the laundry, which draws back - it fastens with a small nail. I came down at half-past eight o'clock the next morning; the things were then gone from the baskets, and the window was a little way open, but not enough to let a person in; the basket had not been near enough to the window to be reached without a stick; the window would open wide enough to let a person in; the nail was quite out of the window.
JOSEPH HUNT. I am a watchman, of Hampstead. I saw the prisoner come down Red Lion-hill, at three o'clock in the morning of the 2d of December, with a bundle; I asked her what she had got; she said two or three things of her own; I took her to the night constable, who found these articles in the bundle.
SAMUEL MUDDOCK. I am a night constable. Hunt brought the prisoner to me about three o'clock; I found this property in the bundle; she said it was her own; I said she would not be dressed as she was if they were; she said I might do as I liked - she seemed very cheerful, and not at all alarmed - I had seen her about ten minutes before twelve o'clock the day before, going towards Hendon- she had nothing then - she was not very sober.
The prisoner put in a petition, representing that she had committed the offence in a state of intoxication.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Of stealing only.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18271206-253]
254. THOMAS MILLGROVE SHEPPARD was indicted for feloniously and burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Henry Fellowes, at St. Mary-le-bone, about eight o'clock in the night of the 1st of December, with intent to steal.
JOHN RAYNER. I am coachman to Mr. William Henry Fellows' mother; Mr. Fellows is now in Norfolk, and was there on the 1st of December; he has a house, No. 15, Lower Berkeley-street - I was taking care of it for him - it is his dwelling-house - I slept there on the night of the 1st of December, and left Irish and Coleman in the dining-room that night - I know nothing of the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know Mr. Fellows' names? A. They are on his letters as Member for Huntingdon.
ROBERT IRISH. I and Coleman were desired by Rayner to watch in this house, on the 1st of December; and about half-past eight o'clock at night, there was a ring at the bell and a knock at the door, which we did not answer, and in about ten minutes there was a violent ringing and knocking; I then left the dining-room, and stood by the street door, with the poker in my hand; I had not been there more than a minute, when the door opened, and the prisoner put one foot over the sill of the door; I seized him by the collar, and he said, "What are you going to do with me?" or words to that effect - he tried to make his escape, and pulled me into the street, as far as the curb - he got from my grasp twice, but I secured him in the carriage-way, by the help of Coleman - I could not see whether any body else was outside.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did he not tell you he was going to give information that he had seenthree men trying to get in? A. I believe he did - he did tell me so - he was searched in my presence; nothing suspicious was found on him except a knife.
COURT. Q. What did he say when you seized him? - A."What are you going to do with me?" he said nothing about giving information, till I gave him in charge of the watchman; and then he said he saw three persons trying to break in, and saw one get over the area - he said there was a key left in the door. I saw no trace of any body else - he did not throw the door wide open, only just to get in; the first ringing was about half-past eight o'clock, and the other about ten minutes after.
WILLIAM COLEMAN. I was desired to keep watch. I went with Irish, and waited in the dining-room; I heard a ringing and knocking at the door about a quarter or twenty minutes past eight o'clock, and shortly afterwards we heard some one in the area; we sat still, and in about ten minutes there came a severe ringing again; we still kept sitting, and Irish said "There is a key going into the door;" he arose and went to the door - I followed him; I saw the door open, and the prisoner's right foot come into the hall; Irish seized him, and they had a scuffle - he dropped the poker: I took it up, and flourished it over the prisoner's head, who was scuffling with Irish in the road; I told him to make no more resistance, and he was given into custody.
Cross-examined. Q. You think there had been a noise in the area after the first ringing? A. Yes; I did not hear the prisoner say there was some people trying the house.
THOMAS HOWARD. I am a watchman. I assisted in taking the prisoner, about twenty minutes before nine o'clock; I went up and saw the scuffle; Irish said."Here is a prisoner for you." I asked what he had been doing; he said he had got into the house - the prisoner did not say any thing - he gave no reason for being there; he asked where we were going to take him; we said to the watch-house; and, when in the watch-house, he said he was watching three men, who were trying to break into the house.
Cross-examined. Q. And did he not say one of those men got into the area? A. I believe he did; he said he saw the key shining in the door - nothing but a large knife was found on him.
COURT. Q. How long had the bell rang before the key was heard in the door? A. It was not more than a minute after the last ring - I think it was half a minute.
Prisoner's Defence. My Lord, and Gentlemen, I beg your attention to the following statement: - I am a painter by trade, and found it very prejudicial to me, and I found I must get another employ; I then opened a shed in the coal and green-grocery line, which my wife is carrying on. On the night in question, I had occasion to go to Oxford-street to purchase some coals; I went to Lower Berkeley-street, and saw two men, whom I knew well, on the steps of the door; I passed on, and they came down and passed me, and met a third person coming towards me, and I heard the two men say to the third, that it would be of no use, they could not get the door open; I went on to the corner of the street, and stood there eight or ten minutes; that might be twenty minutes after eight o'clock; the men came to the corner, went back, and walked the street two or three times; one of them got over the area-gate, and went down the kitchen steps; he came up in a minute, and I should not then have had time to get to the house; a woman came past, and asked what I was waiting for - I said, "Watching three men;" she said she saw, while she was there, a man come out of the area, and that she was going by them, and hoped they would not touch her; we walked by them, and she, as well as I, saw two of their faces, and could swear to them; we walked on to the corner of the square - she said her husband was in the country, and had a six months' job, and she was going down to him, which is the reason I cannot bring her - if I could have got bail I could have gone to the shop her husband works for, and heard where she was - the persons then came from the door and walked entirely away - I watched a few minutes, and then the watchman came up - I waited still, and found they did not return - I then went on to Duke-street, met the three men coming round again, and going towards the house - I followed them, and when they got to the house, two of them ran up the steps - they had not been up three minutes before they came down, walked a few steps, and then ran off - I crossed and rang the bell to tell what I had seen - I then saw the handle of a bright key in the door - I ran up the steps, rang the bell hard, and put my hand on the key - whether I opened it or not I do not know, but I found the door going from me - whether it was open, or I was the occasion of opening it, I do not know, but I thought the persons inside might have pulled the lock back; but I appeal to that Judge, who alone can judge the heart, that my foot was not in the house - the witnesses rushed out - one had a poker - there were repeated blows levelled at me - they broke my hat, and the poker fell - I then told Irish it was of no use, and to desist - the watchman was called, and I given in charge, and at the office made the same defence I have now, and said I had a friend who would give bail for 200l. or 300l. - that I knew the two men, and could leave their description there, and if an officer was granted me, I could find them - the Magistrate said he thought it a fit case to go before a Jury, but if I had friends who would do what I had said, surely they would come forward on my trial, and prove my innocence - I said if I had bail I could produce the woman who knew the two men - I afterwards sent the officer to ask the Magistrate, to grant me bail, which was denied me.
ROBERT IRISH re-examined. He did state that he knew the men by sight; he did not give any description of them, or state their names.
JURY. Q. When did he tell you he was watching the men? A. After I gave him into the watchman's hands - I did not assist in opening the door - he opened it himself, I can swear.
Prisoner. Directly the door was open about eight inches, I felt it suddenly pulled away from me - I had no time to speak to him - I showed no resistance, but to protect myself from his attack - the house had been attempted three Saturdays before; I could have proved I was at home on those occasions.
Three witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 23.
[trialAccount t18271206-254]
255. JOHN ROOK was indicted for stealing, on the 5thof December, at St. Luke, Chelsea, in the dwelling-house, of James Brown, 23 sovereigns, five 2l., and five 1l. Bank notes, his property, against the statute.
JAMES BROWN. I live at No. 4, Queen-street, Chelsea, in the parish of St. Luke, and am a coal-heaver. The prisoner is my son-in-law, and has been to sea; I married his mother - he came to my house when he pleased; he slept there on Monday and Tuesday, the 3d and 4th of December; and on Wednesday, after dinner, he went off, to go to Gravesend. I missed twenty-three sovereigns, and 15l. in Bank notes, on Thursday, when I came home to dinner - it was in a box; I may not have seen it safe for four or five months before - my wife found the box broken open.
JOHN JONES. Between five and six o'clock last Friday, the watchman brought the prisoner to the watch-house, charged with robbing Brown of 38l.; I asked Brown how he knew it was him; he said, "Because nobody else was there;" his wife was not there: he said, that in coming home the prisoner had ran away - he called Stop thief! the watchmen caught him, and they brought him to me - I found seven sovereigns and 16s. 1 1/2d. on him. When I took the charge, the prisoner said he had taken the money at different times; he said, "I took eleven sovereigns last Wednesday;" he said so to Brown, when I was taking the charge.
JAMES BROWN re-examined. I went to Gravesend after him, and brought him back; he offered me seven sovereins to let him go, but did not then say he had taken the money.
Prisoner. I went to look after a ship, and on Thursday my father came and took me.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
[trialAccount t18271206-255]
256. JOHN CHAPMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of November, 1 sheep, price 30s., the property of Richard Franklyn.
SECOND COUNT, for wilfully and feloniously killing the same, with to steal the carcase.
WILLIAM JOHNSON. I am carter to Richard Franklyn, who lives at Totteridge, and has land at Hendon. On the 14th of November, between five and six o'clock at night, I saw all his sheep right - there were twenty: I went next morning, between seven and eight o'clock, and one was gone - I saw some blood on the ground, and a few nuts at the gate of the next field; I saw an ass had been tied to a post, near the gate; I know it was an ass, by the hoof marks; I saw some wool on the little quick hedge, as if a sheep had been driven through. I got a warrant, and went to the prisoner's house at Edgware, and found a leg of mutton in a cupboard - it had been in the pot - it was boiled a little, and all greasy. I found a sheep's head and some fat in a basket - it appeared fresh killed; we found the skin and entrails in the place behind; the skin was not well taken off; they were Leicester long-wool sheep, two years old - we call them two-teethed wether sheep; I found such teeth in the head; I believe the skin to be the skin of master's sheep; and the ear of it, which is marked, is here.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is your master here? A. No; I know he has no other name.
THOMAS PARTRIDGE. I am a farmer, and live in Hendon parish. I went to the prisoner's house with the patrol and constable; he is a green-grocer, and keeps an ass and small cart; we found the cart in the yard, cleanly washed out, and set up to dry; we went into the house, and saw a woman, who said "For God's sake, what do you want?" we found a leg of mutton in the cupboard, and what Johnson has stated.
DANIEL FRANKLYN. I know Richard Franklyn, and his farm - I work on the road as a labourer. On the 14th of November, about a quarter-past eight o'clock in the evening, I met the prisoner leading an ass, drawing a load in a cart; it appeared to me to be bed-clothes - it was on a Tuesday; he was between Edgware and Hendon - he was going towards Hendon, and on the off side of the cart, I saw written, "John Chapman, Little Stanmore," and behind the cart was a man named Samuel Weedon, shoving; he knew me, and said "How are you old man;" I said "How are you?" it was on Tuesday - I cannot say whether it was the 13th or 14th.
STANBRIDGE GINGER. I am a butcher. I went to the prisoner's house, on the 15th of November, with a search-warrant - I found the leg of mutton, with a loin to it, parboiled, it was not killed in a butcher-like manner - I afterwards went, and saw the other sheep in the field; they were all marked, as this is, in the ear, as long-wool sheep generally are; each shepherd marks his own sheep.
WILLIAM JOHNSON. I saw all the sheep safe on Wednesday, the 14th, and missed one next morning; here is the sheep's ear; it has a cut on it; it is the same mark as that on the other nineteen, and there is a mark on the rump of the skin, but it is nasty, as the entrails have been wrapped in it, and it is probably rubbed out; here is the remains of a pitch-mark, and the head was marked with ruddle.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you swear to any mark in the ear? A. Yes.
MR. PHILLIPS to STANBRIDGE GINGER. Q. Are you not an officer? A. Yes - I know Weedon; he is gone away somewhere - I have not looked for him, but other officers have; he lived at Finchley. The prisoner bore a very good character.
Prisoner's Defence. Weedon brought the sheep to my house; he came to me, and I let him a room, and next day took my cart to get his goods - I went into a public-house; Weedon and his brother came in, and sat there from four to ten o'clock; we then got into my cart, and Weedon drove it home, and when we got within a quarter of a mile, he hoisted me out of the cart, as I was tipsy, and asked if I knew what was in it - I said No; he then said, "It is a woolley bird;" I felt in the cart, and said it was a sheep; he said he had picked it up, and it would do for his dogs - I said, next day, "We shall have a piece of work about it;" he said No; he skinned it next day.
STANBRIDGE GINGER, He said nothing about Weedon, when we were at his house.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did he not mention it to Mr. Miller, the Magistrate? A. Yes - somebody was sent to find him.
THOMAS PARTRIDGE. We have a warrant against Weedon respecting this sheep - we searched his house, at Finchley, but found nothing particular there; the prisoner said he had been drinking at a public-house, and got very tipsy - I have known him five or six years, and never knew him in trouble - I have known Weedon in trouble.
NOT GUILTY.
